In a Beijing sculpture workshop at late autumn evening, a 28-year-old Chinese woman with sharp cheekbones, long straight nose, small ears close to skull, and shoulder-length black hair in a low chignon stands with her spine pressed against a cold 364 cm plaster block mounted on a rotating wooden platform. Her bare feet grip the dust-coated concrete floor for balance as she wears only a leather body harness with brass buckles, its straps crossing her ribs and pelvis, the leftover afternoon clay dried in pale streaks across her forearms and collarbones. A single blue gas heater burns in the middle distance, its flame invisible but its heat warping the air above it, while the rest of the workshop sinks into cyan-black shadow where chisels hang on pegboard and half-finished torsos loom on wheeled bases. On-camera flash from a handheld Hasselblad clips to pure white the plaster dust suspended around her head like a halo and freezes her face in sharp relief: her jaw set, mouth slightly open, eyes locked directly into the lens with unwavering challenge, the pupils catching red reflex from the flash tube. Her right hand grips the platform's stained metal rim in fingertips-whitening tension, the knuckles and wrist blurred from the slow rotation she initiated; her left arm extends outward, palm flat against the plaster's rough surface, shoving with visible force so the block turns and her body drags against its inertia. The overdeveloped medium-format negative blows the plaster dust to luminous grain and crushes the workshop corners to dense shadow noise, the sickly warm amber of the gas-heated middle ground bleeding into cold cyan at the frame edges where her shadow stretches long across spilled clay.
